Stated because a review that only lists findings reads as if nothing was checked: the
three constant merges, the seven-site knob wiring (every Life subclass forwards `**knobs`,
so `wander_leash` reaches all of them), `validate_knobs` running before the first packet on
all four inline runners, `_parse_knobs`/`_route_knobs`' prefix handling, the merged popup
stage being behaviour-preserving for `_buy_step` apart from the deliberate counter
reordering, `POPUP_TIMEOUT=50` covering four re-roll cycles at `ASK_RETRY=10`, the
death-edge placement and its first-observation handling, and `min(BANDAGE_GRAPHICS)` with
the rule and gate now sharing one object.

### 15.4 What this does NOT establish

- **Offline only.** The shard is down; none of the five fixes has run on one.
- **Finding 1 is mitigated, not removed.** A tuned leash past the derived reach still
  breaks the chain — by design, so a search can score it — and now says so on the staged
  line. Nobody has watched that line during a real sweep.
- **Findings 2 and 4 were both LATENT.** No shipped runner tunes the overdue gate, and no
  log has ever shown a leaked counter. They are defects by reading, reproduced offline.
- **The review is one pass, not a proof.** It read the diff; it did not run a shard.

1523 → **1527 tests**, ruff clean.

### 15.5 New follow-up

23. **Only the per-trip COUNTERS are cleared at a new goal; the STAGE keys are not.** A
    frame torn down mid-trip still leaves `{ns}_stage`, `{ns}_vendor`, `{ns}_leg` and the
    sell/bank families' equivalents, so the next trip can resume a stage it never entered.
    The general fix is one line per `_begin_goal` (pop all of `_CLEANUP_KEYS`) and it
    breaks six bank tests whose fixtures seed a state production cannot reach. Not taken
    without a live budget: it changes what the flagship path does after a deadline
    expiry, which is exactly the class §6.3 says nothing offline can predict.

## 16. The shard came back, and the week met it (2026-08-07)

`127.0.0.1:2594` answered for the first time in this whole stretch of work — §§9-15 were
all written with it down, and every one of them ends with some form of "offline only".

### 16.1 The outage was ours, and nothing was broken

The first live attempt would have died at the handshake. anima-client had advanced to
**schema 26** (7ca9662) while `ipc_body.SUPPORTED_SCHEMA_VERSION` sat at **18** — eight
versions of drift, accumulated silently, invisible until someone tries to run. CLAUDE.md
already warns that "sibling-repo work on the body lands here as a bump plus a serializer
diff" and that 2026-08-03 spent a live attempt learning it; this time the cost was a
`git diff` instead.

Verified by diffing the serializer, not the changelog: 452 insertions against **seven**
deletions, all seven being the changelog sentence, the constant, a version test, and the
`radio`/`entry` gump-element lines the previous bump already discussed. Cross-checked
mechanically because "I read the diff" is how a rename gets missed — all 19 action `type`
strings `contract.py` emits are still accepted, and all 57 observation keys it reads are
still emitted. The v19-v26 additions (`armed_ability`, `active_spell_icons`, `chat`,
`chat_messages`, `bulletin_board`, `bulletin_message`, ~30 actions) are inert here because
`Observation.from_dict` reads by `d.get(...)`.

Proven rather than asserted, which is this file's own standing complaint about version
numbers: a handshake probe read back `schema_version=26 player=17563` and then a real
observation, `pos=(3734,2222) hits=80/80 dead=False items=45 mobiles=0`.

### 16.2 Three gates, three passes

| gate | result | what it retires |
|---|---|---|
| `live_buy_goal` | **PASSED**, exit 0 | `iron=0->15 gold=200->125 quote=75 opens=1 cancels=0 ticks=12`, `transaction_actions_once=True` |
| `live_toolbuy_goal` | **PASSED**, exit 0 | `tools=0->1 gold=100->87 quote=13 opens=1 cancels=0 ticks=12`, `only_tongs_bought=True` |
| `live_frame_overdue_gate` | **PASSED**, exit 0, all 7 flags | bound 3, reproduced |

The two buy gates are the ones §8.5 recorded as **NOT RUN** — they had never executed with
§8.2's empty-list-cancel filtering, let alone with §13's merged popup stage. The tool-buy
one matters more: it is the FSM that GAINED the entry-edge branch, so it is the half of
§13 that could actually have regressed. `transaction_actions_once=True` on both is the
specific assertion that a trailing cancel would break.

**Bound 3 reproduced the 2026-08-03 run almost tick for tick** — overdue at economy tick
**301** against `deadline_tick=300`, `_repair_overdue_frame` closing the craft FSM's own
gump on that tick (`closing an unowned gump id=2066278152`), the hold extended **exactly
one** economy tick and then released into `mode=hunt` with the frame still live and still
overdue, after **299** consecutive held ticks, with `craft_steps_seen=[2]` proving the FSM
stayed starved. That is the documented worst case, unchanged. It also clears review finding
2 by construction: this is the gate whose mid-run re-leash invariant that finding was about,
and it passes with the corrected precedence.

### 16.3 What three green gates do NOT establish

The temptation after a clean live session is to read it as "the week is validated". It is
not, and the gaps are specific:

- **`cancels=0` on both buy gates.** The first window carried the offer both times, so the
  re-roll path, the `{ns}_closing_window` marker, the foreign-window branch and the cancel
  re-send — §13's and §15's actual subject matter — were **never entered**. Every claim
  about them is still offline. Reaching them needs an unlucky partial subset, which is
  luck, or a staged fixture, which does not exist.
- **Bound 1 is still unexercised**, exactly as §6.3, §7.4 and §8.3 leave it. Nothing here
  produced a transaction that FAILED.
- **The death readout (§9) has never fired.** No agent died; `deaths=0` is all these runs
  can say, and a field that has only ever printed 0 is not a tested field.
- **No tuned knob ran.** §E's criterion — a tuned value changing a live trajectory — is
  untouched by all three gates, which run untuned by design.
- **The forge pair did not run**, so the seven-site knob wiring (§10), the leash axis (§11)
  and the banner work (§14) have still never printed on a shard.
- **These are GATES, not a day.** They are minutes long and staged closed. The 1800-tick
  failures this project actually learns from — the silent miner, the wedged window — live
  in long runs, and none happened here.

1527 tests, ruff clean. Schema 18 → **26**.

## 17. §E's criterion, met — a tuned knob changed a live trajectory (2026-08-07)

`python -m anima2.village --forge-pair --knob bank_trip_surplus=10 --ticks 1200`, on the
flagship miner→tinker chain. **Predictions were written down before the run** (kept in the
session scratchpad) precisely so this could be refuted rather than narrated.

### 17.1 The prediction, and what it demanded

`TinkerLife.decide_mode`'s URGENT bank branch — the one that outranks even a ready craft:

    if gold > bank_reserve + bank_trip_surplus:  ->  economy/bank_gold
        default : gold > 82 + 75 = 157
        tuned   : gold > 82 + 10 =  92

So a `bank_gold` admission anywhere in **93..157** is a decision the default rule could not
have made. Tuning DOWN was chosen deliberately: 2026-08-03's live knob run was
behaviourally INERT because the carpenter's gold never reached the tuned value, and a lower
bar cannot fail that way.

### 17.2 All three confirmed

1. **The channel carried it.** `staged: Grimm@(2611,474) -> drop (2609, 474) ->
   Pim@(2609,474) (reserve 82, trip surplus 10, leash 1, both broke)` — `10`, not the
   module default 75. First time any of §10's five newly-wired construction sites has
   carried a tuned value on a shard.
2. **The value STEERED.** Nine `bank_gold` admissions, **six of them at gold in the
   93..157 band** — 105, 112, 112, 117, 117, 117. At every one of those the default rule
   says *keep crafting*. (The three at 82 are mid-trip samples, taken after the coin had
   already moved to the box.)
3. **More banking than the untuned baseline.** 9 deposits and **293g banked** over 1200
   ticks, against the recorded untuned 1200-tick run's **193g**. Net **+410g**.

**This is AUTONOMY-ROADMAP §E's criterion, word for word — "every searched axis changes a
meaningful live trajectory" — met for the first time, on one axis.** CLAUDE.md's
precondition (a) has said "no tuned knob has ever changed a live trajectory" since it was
written. It no longer does.

### 17.3 Three more things printed on a shard for the first time

- **§9's death readout**: `Grimm miner @(2609,486) t=886 hp=83/83 deaths=0`. Exercised,
  **not tested** — `deaths=0` on every sample of both agents, so the edge path is still
  unfired and a field that has only printed 0 proves only that it prints.
- **§14's `leash_readout`**: `leash 1`, correctly bare — a home is set (not `inert`) and
  the value is untuned (no `TUNED over`). Both of that function's branches stayed silent
  because both should have.
- **§8.1's work-liveness alarm, firing TRULY on the flagship pair**: `NO OUTPUT for 240
  ticks (eps=41 unchanged since t=452, skill=buy_iron)`, escalating to 480, with
  `!stalled` on 41 samples. It named the culprit skill correctly.

Clean on everything else: **0 deaths, 0 `!frozen`, 0 `FRAME OVERDUE`, 0 rule-vs-gate
disagreements, 0 `closing an unowned`**, and `ui=shopbuy` on 4 of 138 samples (against
75/208 before the wedge fix).

### 17.4 What the run found that the prediction did not — follow-up 19, measured

`retired=44:41a/3x`. The three expiries are **all `buy_iron`, all at exactly `180/180`**,
and the shape between them is unmistakable: **55 samples with `buy_iron` ADMITTED,
`ready=['buy_iron']`, and `mkt_phase=craft`** — gold pinned at 77, iron at 1, with **no
`+hold`, no `!frozen`, no `!overdue`** on any of them. The rule wants it, the gate allows
it, the frame owns the stack, the economy agent is being ticked, and nothing executes.

The mechanism is not a mystery and it is not new: `_buy_step` ran its trip (4 samples at
`mkt_phase=buy`, `buy_stage=popup`), gave up, and returned. The give-up sets
`cap_buy_finished_goal_id` — which only the buy's own achievement check reads — and **not**
`cap_run_finished_goal_id`, the marker `CapabilityGoalComplete`'s give-up branch keys on.
`market.py` sets that marker on the SELL path (:1681) and the BANK path (:2072) and nowhere
on either buy path. So a given-up buy frame has no way to retire except by outliving its
own deadline.

**That is follow-up 19, verbatim, and its stated deferral reason is now discharged.** §8.6
declined it because "it is a behaviour change on that path that nobody has measured". It is
measured: **3 frames × 180 ticks = 540 dead economy ticks, 45% of a 1200-tick run**, on the
one positive-margin chain this project has. The audit also predicted the diagnostic:
"a run carrying it prints `FRAME RETIRED buy_iron#N … -> giveup` where today it prints
nothing" — today it printed `-> expired` three times instead, which is the same frame
taking 180 ticks to say what bound 1 would have said in a few.

It would also give **bound 1 its first live exercise**, which §6.3, §7.4, §8.3 and §16.3
all record as missing.

### 17.5 What this run still does NOT establish

- **One axis, one value, one run.** `bank_trip_surplus=10` steered; nothing here says the
  other four knobs do, and there is no A/B — the untuned comparison is a run from a
  different day at a different tick count, so "293 vs 193" is indicative, not controlled.
- **The steer was not shown to be GOOD.** More banking is not more profit, and the same
  run starved the tinker's working capital down to 77 gold against a 75-gold iron batch.
  Whether the tuned value CAUSED the buy stalls is **not proven** — the trips died in the
  popup stage, not on affordability — and asserting it would be exactly the
  unmeasured-cause claim this file keeps having to retract.
- **The re-roll path and the `closing_window` marker were still not exercised.**
  `closing an unowned` never printed, `buy_stage` only ever read `popup`, so §13's and
  §15's subject matter remains offline-only after two live sessions.
- **Bound 1 is STILL unexercised.** Three buy frames gave up and all three retired through
  bound 2 instead, which is the defect above, not the proof.

1527 tests, ruff clean.

## 18. Follow-up 19 applied — and the re-run did NOT prove it (2026-08-09)

The fix is small and its offline evidence is solid. The live re-run is the part that has to
be reported carefully, because the temptation is to read "0 expiries" as success.

### 18.1 The change

`market.py` writes the neutral `cap_run_finished_goal_id` at the end of both buy families'
return phases, exactly where the sell (:1681) and bank (:2072) wrappers have written it
since forge1/forge13. Set UNCONDITIONALLY, because `CapabilityGoalComplete` tests
`_achieved` FIRST and returns SUCCESS there — it is the give-up branch's KEY, not a
give-up flag.

Offline: a `_wedged_buy_life` frame that used to ride to `180/180` now retires
`-> giveup` at **age 17**. Mutation-checked precisely (removing only the two new markers,
leaving sell and bank alone): 1 test fails. Live safety check: `live_buy_goal` re-run
after the change still reports `exact_goal_frame_succeeded_once = True`, so an achieved
buy still retires as achieved.

### 18.2 Four bound-2 tests were standing on the defect

`_wedged_buy_life`'s docstring named its own third leg: *"a BUY frame has no give-up ladder
at all"*. With the marker that is false, and the world closes through bound 1 long before
its deadline — so four tests that used it as a bound-2 vehicle broke. They moved to
`_expiring_craft_life`: a CRAFT frame, because `CraftItemCapability` still writes no
marker, whose material vanishes mid-craft so it can neither achieve nor self-close. It
retires `expired` at exactly `300/300`. That is the same recipe the live bound-3 gate
stages, which is why it is trustworthy — a shard has produced the shape.

Keeping those tests on a buy frame would have meant preserving the defect in order to keep
testing around it.

One experiment discarded, recorded because it looks plausible and is wrong: starving the
buy FSM by wounding the character to 12 HP does NOT give a bound-2 world. It starves
`expire_due` too — that runs inside `Agent.tick` — which is bound 3's livelock, not
bound 2's deadline.

### 18.3 The re-run, and why it settles nothing about this fix

Same command, same knob, same tick count as §17:

| | before (§17) | after |
|---|---|---|
| samples | 138 | 142 |
| banked / net | 293g / +410g | 233g / +315g |
| retirements | `44:41a/3x` | `27:27a` |
| bound 2 (expired) | 3 × `buy_iron` at `180/180` | **0** |
| bound 1 (giveup) | 0 | **0** |
| dead ticks in expired buy frames | **540** | 0 |
| **samples with `buy_iron` admitted** | 60 | **0** |

**The last row is the one that matters. The tinker never needed to buy iron at all in the
second run** — Grimm's delivery covered it — so the code path this fix changes was never
entered. The zero expiries are zero because there were no buy frames, not because the
marker closed them. **Bound 1 remains unexercised live**, exactly as §6.3, §7.4, §8.3,
§16.3 and §17.5 all leave it.

The economics differ too (233g vs 293g), and that is supply variance, not the fix: two runs
on different mining luck are not an A/B. Nothing here should be attributed to follow-up 19
in either direction.

What the re-run DOES show is no regression: 27 retirements, every one achieved, zero
expiries, zero deaths, one `NO OUTPUT` against three, and the pair ran its full day.

### 18.4 Bound 1 needs a purpose-built gate, not patience

This is the same conclusion §7.1 reached for bound 3, and for the same reason. Reaching
the give-up path needs a buy that FAILS — an unlucky partial subset, or a vendor that
cannot sell — and a forge run produces one only by chance. §7 answered that for bound 3 by
building `live_frame_overdue_gate.py` with forced state; bound 1 needs its equivalent, and
two forge runs have now failed to produce it by luck (60 admitted buy samples and three
expiries in one, zero buy frames at all in the other).

**Follow-up 24 — CLOSED the same day, see §19. It passed first attempt, all eleven flags.** Stage a vendor with no iron offer — the
condition `OFFER_REOPEN_ATTEMPTS` exists for — so the trip exhausts its re-rolls, gives up,
walks home and writes the marker. It would print `FRAME RETIRED buy_iron#N … -> giveup`,
which is bound 1's first live signature, and it would exercise the re-roll path and the
`{ns}_closing_window` marker that §13, §15, §16.3 and §17.5 all record as never having run
on a shard. One gate, four gaps.

1528 tests, ruff clean.

## 19. Bound 1, LIVE — the give-up ladder on a shard (2026-08-09)

`anima2/live_buy_giveup_gate.py`, follow-up 24. **First attempt, exit 0, all eleven flags.**

```
GM staged a smith with NO iron and a Healer that sells none; subject=0x44B0 gold=200 vendor=17585
[FLAG] BUY GIVE-UP GATE (bound 1) PASSED: vendor=17585 rerolls=4/4 cancels=5 iron=0
       deadline_tick=182 retired=(1, 'buy_ingots', 21, 180, 'giveup')
```

### 19.1 What it forced, and why patience could not

§7.1 established the principle for bound 3 and it holds here: **reaching a bound needs
forced state, not patience.** Two 1200-tick forge runs failed to produce a failing buy —
the first admitted `buy_iron` on 60 samples and retired three frames on their DEADLINE
(§17.4), the second never needed to buy at all (§18.3).

The forced state is ONE substitution, and nothing is injected: `live_buy_goal.py` stages a
**Blacksmith**, whose stock includes iron, and its trip succeeds. This stages a **Healer**
at the same spot — a real vendor, a real `Buy` context entry, a real shop window, stocking
bandages and no iron. The FSM meets a world in which its offer genuinely does not exist,
which is the condition `OFFER_REOPEN_ATTEMPTS` was written for. No memory is hand-written
and no gump is faked.

### 19.2 Four gaps closed by one run

| gap | recorded as missing in | now |
|---|---|---|
| **Bound 1 — the give-up ladder, live** | §6.3, §7.4, §8.3, §16.3, §17.5, §18.3 | `retired=(1, 'buy_ingots', 21, 180, 'giveup')` |
| The partial-subset **re-roll path** | §13, §15, §16.3, §17.5 | `rerolls=4/4` |
| `{ns}_closing_window`, §15's marker | §15.4, §16.3, §17.5 | `closing_window_marker_set = True` |
| Follow-up 19's marker, live | §18.3 | `giveup_marker_written = True` |

**Bound 1 had been named as unexercised in six sections across eleven days.** It is
exercised.

**The payoff is the two numbers side by side: age 21 against a 180-tick budget.** The
frame closed in 12% of its deadline. Before follow-up 19 this exact shape burned the whole
180 — measured at three frames and 540 dead ticks, 45% of a 1200-tick run (§17.4).

`cancels=5` is arithmetic worth checking rather than glossing: four re-roll closes, one
exit-edge close on the give-up. That is §8.2(c)'s "a trip cancels the window it opened"
firing on a shard for the first time as well, and `nothing_was_bought` confirms every one
of the five was ServUO's EndVendorBuy rather than a purchase — the distinction two live
gates once got wrong (§8.2).

### 19.3 What the gate deliberately does NOT prove

- **It does not prove the flagship chain no longer wastes ticks.** It proves the mechanism
  works when the path is entered. §18.3's re-run still never entered it, and no ordinary
  forge day has yet retired a buy frame through bound 1.
- **It is a closed fixture**, like every gate here: one capability ready, one vendor, no
  miner, no competing goals. A give-up under real contention is not tested.
- **`OFFER_REOPEN_ATTEMPTS = 4` is still unvalidated as a NUMBER.** The gate shows all four
  attempts are spent and bounded; it says nothing about whether four is the right count for
  the real ~15-of-45-entry pairing bug, because this vendor never had the offer at all.
- **No offline reproduction.** The bound-3 gate has seven (§7.5); this has none, because
  reproducing it needs a `MockBody` that can answer a vendor window — which is follow-up
  22, deferred on the argument that "the wedge is covered by FSM-level tests plus a scratch
  harness". That argument is weaker now: this gate is the second live artifact that cannot
  be reproduced offline for want of the same fixture.

1528 tests, ruff clean. Follow-up 24 is closed; 22 is upgraded by it.

## 20. Follow-up 22 — a vendor in `MockBody`, and the gate reproduced offline (2026-08-09)

Follow-up 22 was deferred twice, on the argument that *"adding a vendor to `MockBody` is a
production-code change for a test's benefit; the wedge is covered by FSM-level tests plus
that harness"*. What made it finally worth doing is that the argument kept getting weaker:

- §8.2's Life-level wedge reproduction was run from a **scratch harness** instead, which no
  longer exists;
- §19's bound-1 gate — the **only** live proof of the give-up ladder this project has —
  shipped with **no offline reproduction at all**, where the bound-3 gate has seven (§7.5).

Two live artifacts blocked on one missing fixture. And `MockBody`'s own docstring calls it
*"a test double"*: it is test infrastructure that happens to live in the package, so
"production-code change" was always the weaker half of the framing.

### 20.1 `windows` is a list of OPENINGS, not a stock list

This is the whole design and it is the one decision worth defending. ServUO shows a
vendor's goods in partial SUBSETS — one opening can lack an item the next one carries,
which is the ~15-of-45-entry pairing bug `OFFER_REOPEN_ATTEMPTS` exists for. So
`MockVendor.windows` is a list of entry lists, cycled per opening:

- **one element** → every opening identical. That is §19's Healer: no amount of re-rolling
  can turn up an offer the vendor does not have.
- **several** → a re-roll genuinely finds what the first window lacked.

Modelling a flat stock instead would have made the re-roll path **untestable by
construction** — the exact gap this class exists to close.

`_settle_purchase` moves goods in and coin out and does no more: under-payment and stock
depletion are server rules, and a double that enforced them would be asserting a contract
this project does not own.

### 20.2 The bound-1 gate, offline

`tests/test_mock_vendor.py` drives the real `_buy_step` against a vendor stocking bandages
and no iron — §19's staging, with nothing injected into memory — and asserts the trip ENDS,
spends its whole re-roll budget, REOPENS the window rather than re-reading one snapshot,
cancels rather than buys, and leaves both the pack and the purse untouched. The control
beside it stocks the offer and requires the buy to still complete, because without it the
give-up test would pass just as well against an FSM that could no longer buy anything.

Mutation-checked: making the re-roll emit no action — the pre-8.2 behaviour that
"re-rolled NOTHING", since a window is a snapshot — fails it.

*A process note worth more than the test.* The first mutation run reported PASS and the
fix looked unverified-but-fine; the pattern had simply not matched and no mutant was ever
applied. A mutation test that does not assert it mutated something is a green light
measuring nothing.

### 20.3 One fixture bug, recorded because it is invisible

The control test first failed with `bought=0`, which reads exactly like a broken FSM. It
was the fixture: the vendor's FOR-SALE display graphic is not one of `INGOT_GRAPHICS` —
those are the pack pile-size variants a bought stack merges into, a distinction
`live_buy_goal._pack_iron` already carries in its docstring. Getting it wrong makes the FSM
never find its offer and re-roll, which is indistinguishable from the give-up being tested.
The fixture now reads `BuyIron.buy_offer_graphic` off the skill so it cannot drift.

### 20.4 What this does NOT do

- ~~**The FRAME half of §19 is still not reproduced.**~~ **DONE the same day — §20.5.**
- **It is inert for every existing fixture.** `vendors` defaults empty and `popup` to
  `None`, so all 1528 prior tests pass unchanged — which is the evidence that nothing was
  quietly re-specified, not a claim that the vendor is right.
- **It models no server rule.** No stock depletion, no affordability refusal, no
  price haggling. A test that needs one is asserting ServUO's behaviour, and this double
  cannot answer for it.

1528 → **1534 tests**, ruff clean. Follow-up 22 is closed.

### 20.5 The frame half, and it lands within two ticks of the shard

§20.4 named the gap: those tests drive `_buy_step`, and a `-> giveup` RETIREMENT needs a
Life with a goal stack. `_tinker_that_can_only_buy` is that Life — one whose rule can reach
exactly ONE branch, closed by construction rather than by luck: a tool (so
`buy_tinker_tool` is unready), no tongs (no `sell_tongs`), no iron anywhere (no
`craft_tongs`, no `fetch_iron`), and **no `banker_spot` at all**, which closes BOTH bank
branches without depending on a gold threshold a knob could move.

Driven over the same vendor, the retirement rows are:

| world | retirement | vendor openings |
|---|---|---|
| bandages, no iron (§19's Healer) | `(1, 'buy_iron', **19**, 180, 'giveup')` | 5 |
| stocks iron (control) | `(1, 'buy_iron', 7, 180, 'achieved')` | 1 |

**The live gate's verdict was `(1, 'buy_ingots', 21, 180, 'giveup')`.** Offline 19 against
live 21, on the same 180-tick budget — a two-tick difference across a real shard, a real
bridge and a real ServUO vendor. That correspondence is the actual result here: it says the
double models the exchange faithfully, which is the only thing that makes an offline
reproduction worth having. Five vendor openings matches the live `cancels=5` (one initial
opening plus four re-rolls, against four re-roll closes plus the exit-edge close).

The control is the load-bearing half for follow-up 19's SAFETY, not its benefit: the marker
is written unconditionally, so if `CapabilityGoalComplete` ever stopped testing achievement
first, every successful buy would start reporting as a give-up and the bound-1 signal three
audit sections were spent building would mean nothing. Live that assertion is
`live_buy_goal`'s `exact_goal_frame_succeeded_once`; offline it is now this row.

Mutation-checked with the mutant VERIFIED to have applied (§20.2's lesson): reverting
follow-up 19 — removing exactly the two buy markers and leaving sell and bank alone — makes
the give-up test fail.

**So bound 1 is now covered at three levels**: the FSM (`_buy_step` against the mock), the
frame (a Life retiring through the ladder), and a shard (§19's gate). It was covered at
none of them eleven days ago.

1534 → **1536 tests**, ruff clean.

## 21. Regression sweep — four live gates after the vendor work (2026-08-09)

`MockBody` is a test double and cannot reach a shard, but `market.py` had been changed the
same day (follow-up 19) and the gates had not been re-run since. Four gates, four passes,
exit 0 each.

| gate | verdict |
|---|---|
| `live_buy_goal` | `iron=0->15 gold=200->125 opens=1 cancels=0`, `exact_goal_frame_succeeded_once` |
| `live_toolbuy_goal` | `tools=0->1 gold=100->87 opens=1 cancels=0`, `only_tongs_bought` |
| `live_buy_giveup_gate` | `rerolls=4/4 cancels=5 iron=0 retired=(1, 'buy_ingots', 21, 180, 'giveup')` |
| `live_frame_overdue_gate` | all 7 flags; `overdue=(299, 301) repair=(299, 301) released=(300, 302) extension=1` |

### 21.1 The forced-state gates are DETERMINISTIC, and that is the finding

Both forced-state gates reproduced their previous verdicts **field for field**:

```
bound 1, run 1: rerolls=4/4 cancels=5 iron=0 deadline_tick=182 retired=(1, 'buy_ingots', 21, 180, 'giveup')
bound 1, run 2: rerolls=4/4 cancels=5 iron=0 deadline_tick=182 retired=(1, 'buy_ingots', 21, 180, 'giveup')

bound 3, run 1: overdue=(299, 301) repair=(299, 301) released=(300, 302) extension=1 closes_while_overdue=1 hold_ticks_before_overdue=299 craft_steps_seen=[2]
bound 3, run 2: overdue=(299, 301) repair=(299, 301) released=(300, 302) extension=1 closes_while_overdue=1 hold_ticks_before_overdue=299 craft_steps_seen=[2]
```

Different accounts, different vendor serials, different shard sessions, identical numbers.
That matters more than the passes: **it is what makes these gates regression DETECTORS
rather than anecdotes.** A forge run's numbers swing with mining luck — §17 banked 293g and
§18 banked 233g on the same command — so a forge run can only ever say "something happened".
A gate whose verdict is a fixed string can say "this specific thing still happens", and a
future change that shifts age 21 to age 180 will read as a diff rather than as noise.

It also retro-justifies §7.1's insistence on forced state over patience: the same property
that makes a bound reachable on demand is what makes it comparable across runs.

### 21.2 What the sweep does NOT cover

- **No forge day.** The four gates are closed fixtures, minutes long. The 1800-tick failure
  shapes this project actually learns from — the silent miner, the wedged window, the buy
  stall of §17.4 — are not exercised by any of them.
- **The sell and bank families were not re-run.** Follow-up 19 did not touch them (their
  markers predate it by weeks) and no gate here covers them, so "no regression" is scoped
  to the buy paths and the hold.
- **`MockBody`'s vendor is not live-verifiable at all**, by construction. §20.5's two-tick
  agreement between the offline reproduction and the shard is the only evidence that the
  double is faithful, and it is evidence about one exchange, not about the double.

1536 tests, ruff clean.

## 22. A real day — follow-up 19 in production, and the miner died silently a third time (2026-08-10)

`--forge-pair --knob bank_trip_surplus=10 --ticks 1800`, predictions written down first.
1800 rather than 1200 because §18's shorter run never entered the buy path at all, and
because 1800 is the historical baseline (§8.1).

*(The first attempt aborted with `login failed: connection closed by server` on the second
spawn. Probed rather than assumed: a brand-new account logs in fine, and four concurrent
connections open fine, so it was neither an account cap nor a connection cap — a transient
refusal that a retry cleared. Recorded because "the pair needs both; aborting" is the same
message a knob typo would produce, and this one was not that.)*

### 22.1 Follow-up 19, in production: 49 give-ups, ZERO expiries

```
retired=107:58a/49g
buy_iron -> giveup : 49, EVERY ONE at age 17/180
buy_iron -> expired:  0
```

§17.4, before the fix, on the same command at 1200 ticks: **three** `buy_iron` frames, all
at `180/180`. Now zero frames reach bound 2 and every give-up costs **17 economy ticks
instead of 180** — a 10.6x reduction per frame, live, on the flagship chain.

**A correction to my own intermediate reading.** Mid-run I computed "7987 ticks not burned"
from `49 × (180 - 17)`. That is a bad counterfactual and it is dropped: 49 frames at 180
ticks each is 8820 economy ticks, which does not fit in this run at all — the pre-fix world
would have run FEWER buy attempts, not the same 49 more slowly. The defensible statements
are the two above: 17 versus 180 per frame, and 0 versus 3-of-3 reaching the deadline.

### 22.2 Why the buys failed, measured rather than guessed

49 give-ups against 2 successes invites "the partial-subset pairing bug". It is not. The
two successes are the **first two** buy frames of the run (#13 and #21, positions 0 and 1
of 51); every one of the 49 that follows gave up. That ordering is stock depletion — the
vendor sold its iron and stayed dry — not an unlucky subset, which would scatter successes
throughout.

So the give-ups are **correct behaviour**: the tinker repeatedly discovers there is no iron
for sale. Follow-up 19's value is exactly that it makes discovering it cost 17 ticks
instead of 180.

**New follow-up 25: nothing tells the tinker the vendor is dry.** It re-admitted a hopeless
`buy_iron` 49 times in one day. Each attempt is cheap now, which is precisely why this was
invisible before — at 180 ticks apiece the run could not have made 49 of them. A vendor
that has answered "no stock" N times running is a world-fact the rule could hold, and
holding it would free those ticks for `fetch_iron`, the free-material branch that outranks
buying.

### 22.3 The knob steered again, and the economics landed on the baseline

Eleven `bank_gold` admissions at pack gold in the 93..157 band (112, 117 × 10) — decisions
the default `gold > 157` rule could not make. §17's six, re-confirmed on a longer day.

`banked=503 net=+585g`, which is **identical to the 2026-08-03 1800-tick baseline** (§8.1:
"banked 503g (+585g net) over 1800 ticks"). Prices, batch sizes and the craft/sell cycle
are all fixed, so an 1800-tick day converging on the same figure is plausible rather than
suspicious — but it is a coincidence of two samples, not a demonstration of determinism,
and it is recorded as the former.

### 22.4 The miner stopped producing — for the THIRD recorded time — and the alarm said so

```
** Grimm: NO OUTPUT for 240 ticks (eps=69 unchanged since t=1061, skill=mine_smelt_deliver) **
   ...escalating to 480 and 720
Grimm  miner  @(2604,499) t=1800 hp=83/83 deaths=0 out+191.5 eps=69 steps=199 says=0 !stalled  [BUDGET SPENT · STALLED ...]
```

Grimm's ledger froze at `eps=69` at t=1061 and never moved again — 59 of 209 samples
`!stalled`, 41% of the day. This is the shape that went unflagged in 2026-07-30's run and
again in 2026-08-03's (§8.1), and **this time the tape said so within 240 ticks**, named
the culprit skill, escalated, and carried the stall into the terminal line.

`hp=83/83 deaths=0` throughout says what the alarm alone could not: he did not die. So the
distinction §9 was built for — a corpse versus a lost pickaxe versus a dead vein — is now
readable, and this run reads "not a corpse". Which of the other two it is, the tape still
cannot say.

*A second correction.* I first reported `!stalled` on 59 samples as if it were the tinker's.
It is GRIMM's worker line; Pim's is `!stalled` on 0 of 209 and ends `eps=146`. The pair
status line carries no `!stalled` at all. Counting a string across a whole log and
attributing it to whichever agent is under discussion is how a reading goes wrong.

### 22.5 The rest of the health sheet

`DIED` 0, `BACK ALIVE` 0, `FRAME OVERDUE` 0, `!frozen` 0, `RULE-vs-GATE` 0. `ui=shopbuy` on
29 of 209 samples — higher than §17's 4, and expected: 49 buy trips each open a window and
close it. No trip left one behind, since `FRAME OVERDUE` and the wedge signature are both
absent.

### 22.6 What this day did NOT settle

- **The death readout is still only exercised, not tested.** Three live sessions, zero
  deaths, `deaths=0` on every sample of both agents.
- **`OFFER_REOPEN_ATTEMPTS = 4` remains unvalidated as a number.** This vendor had no stock
  at all, so re-rolling could not have helped and the count was never under test.
- **Grimm's stall is diagnosed no further than "not a death".** Whether it is a worn
  pickaxe or an exhausted vein is exactly what §9 said the tape still cannot distinguish.
- **The economics are one sample.** 503g matching the baseline is two runs agreeing, and
  the audit has been wrong before about what a matching number proves.

1536 tests, ruff clean.

## 23. Follow-up 25 — a vendor that just proved it is dry (2026-08-10)

§22.2's finding: a tinker whose vendor sold out after two purchases re-admitted `buy_iron`
**49 more times** in one 1800-tick day. Every give-up was correct — there really was no
iron — and "this vendor is dry" was a world-fact nothing carried from one trip to the next.

It was invisible until follow-up 19 made a failed buy cost 17 ticks instead of 180: at the
old price the run could not have fitted 49 of them. **Fixing one thing is what made the
next thing measurable.**

### 23.1 The mechanism, and where it deliberately is NOT

The FSM writes `{ns}_dry_until` on **one** give-up path: the offer absent from every window
after the re-roll budget is spent. That is the only one meaning "this vendor does not stock
it" — a menu that never arrived, or a vendor never found, are different world-facts and
must not stand a good shop down.

The RULE reads it, through `market.vendor_dry`, the single read point. **Deliberately not a
capability gate**: a gate refusing here while the rule still wanted the buy would be a
manufactured rule-vs-gate disagreement, and the detector built to catch those would fire
every time a shop ran out — the very thing this project spends most of its live budget
chasing. The rule simply stops asking, which the concordance suite confirms is safe (its
assertion is "never WANT what the gate refuses"; wanting less cannot violate it).

`VENDOR_DRY_BACKOFF = 180` is derived, not picked: one buy frame's own deadline, on the
rule that a failed trip should get at least as long to become worth retrying as a trip is
given to succeed.

Wired on **all four** material-buy branches — tinker `buy_iron`, carpenter `buy_boards`,
warrior `buy_bandage`, mage `buy_reagent` — not only the tinker where it was measured.
One Life fixed and three not is the defect class §12 and §13 are both instances of, and a
test asserts all four consult it.

### 23.2 The first version was a permanent stand-down wearing a timer

It measured the backoff in `mkt_tick`, which only advances inside a market skill's `step()`.
Standing the buy branch down sends the Life to hunt mode → the economy agent stops ticking →
`mkt_tick` stops → **the expiry is never reached.** Measured on the offline Life:

```
after ~ 200 ticks: mkt_tick=18 dry_until=196 econ_ticks=19
after ~ 600 ticks: mkt_tick=18 dry_until=196 econ_ticks=19
after ~1200 ticks: mkt_tick=18 dry_until=196 econ_ticks=19
```

Frozen. A rule that never asks again, dressed as a 180-tick pause.

**It is the same shape this file has already recorded twice**: §5 on `expire_due` ("frozen,
the deadline is unreachable by construction — 180 econ ticks never arrive when the econ
agent stops at 5") and §18.2's discarded bound-2 experiment (wounding starves `expire_due`
along with the FSM). **A threshold must never be counted in a clock the decision it gates
can halt.**

The fix is `life_tick`, stamped by `WarriorLife.tick` from `hunt_agent.ticks +
econ_agent.ticks` — exactly one inner agent ticks per orchestrator tick, so it advances by
1 every tick whatever the mode. `market.market_clock` is the one read, preferring it and
falling back to `mkt_tick` for a bare `Agent`, whose buy is chosen by the gate rather than
by a rule that can stand down.

**How it was caught matters more than the fix.** The first measurement said "1 hopeless
trip in 1800 ticks against 49" — a 49x improvement, which is exactly the sort of number
worth distrusting. It was not an improvement; it was the branch switched off.

### 23.3 Measured, after

```
1800 Life ticks against a dry vendor -> 9 hopeless trips   (the live day made 49)
life_tick=1806  dry_until=1802   — the stand-down expires and the vendor is rechecked
```

A 5.4x reduction with the shop still rechecked about every 180 ticks, so a restock is
noticed rather than waited out forever.

Mutation-checked: reverting `market_clock` to the halt-able clock fails 2 tests; removing
the FSM's marker fails 2; unwiring one of the four rules fails the symmetry test.

### 23.4 What this does NOT establish

- **Offline only.** No live run has carried it. The 49→9 figure is the offline Life against
  the mock vendor, and the live number will differ with the shard's own restock timing.
- **180 is derived, not validated.** Nothing measures whether a real ServUO vendor restocks
  faster or slower than that; too long wastes a restock, too short wastes trips.
- **It does not make the tinker do anything better with the freed ticks.** The rule falls
  through to `hunt` (a supply wait). Whether those ticks could feed `fetch_iron` or a craft
  is untested, and the live day's chain was miner-limited anyway (§22.4).
- **The tool-buy family writes the marker but no rule reads it.** `buy_tinker_tool`,
  `buy_weapon`, `buy_armor`, `buy_hatchet` and `buy_saw` are tool branches; their FSM marks
  a dry vendor and nothing consults it yet. Named rather than half-wired — follow-up 26.

1536 → **1542 tests**, ruff clean.

## 24. Schema 27, and a day that proved nothing about follow-up 25 (2026-08-10)

### 24.1 The lockstep guard fired, in the open

The forge run aborted at the handshake: `unsupported bridge schema 27; expected 26`, both
logins refused, nothing staged, no half-contract day. The sibling repo had moved to 27
(anima-client 91e3dc3) mid-session. **That is the guard working, not a failure** — §16.1
paid for the same drift with an eight-version gap; this cost a retry.

26→27 verified by diffing, as always. FIVE deletions, four of them the changelog sentence,
the constant and a version test. The fifth is why the diff is read rather than the
changelog — it is inside `player_json`, the object carrying `dead` that §9's death readout
is built on:

```
-  "body": p.body, "poisoned": p.poisoned, "dead": p.dead,
+  "body": p.body, "poisoned": p.poisoned, "dead": p.dead, "race": p.race,
```

Purely additive; `body`, `poisoned`, `dead` untouched. Cross-checked mechanically (19
action types still accepted, 57 observation keys still emitted) and proven on the shard:
`ready schema_version=27`, then an observation reading `hits=80/80 dead=False body=400
poisoned=False` — the exact fields that line touches.

*(One mechanical false positive chased down rather than waved off: `PlayerView.pos` reads
as "missing from the bridge" under a naive `"key": p.field` scan, because it is emitted as
a nested `pos_json` object. A false positive in a mechanical check is exactly as bad as a
false negative.)*

### 24.2 The day: NO DATA for 49→9, and the prediction said so in advance

```
retired=3:3a   banked=0   net=+35g   samples=209
buy_iron: 0 admitted, 0 give-ups, 0 achieved
pack gold peaked at 35 — an iron batch costs 75
```

**The buy branch was never wanted, so the backoff was never exercised.** This is precisely
the outcome the written-beforehand prediction named as no-data rather than success:
*"If this run's tinker never exhausts the shop — or never needs to buy at all — the branch
is never stood down and the comparison says nothing."*

Reporting it as "0 give-ups, down from 49" would be the single most dishonest reading
available in this document, and it is the one the numbers invite.

### 24.3 Why: the miner delivered ONE pile, all day

```
grimm[iron=0] on 209 of 209 samples;  drop pim_sees=10 on ONE sample
Grimm  ... out+99.6 eps=72 steps=213  !stalled on 64 of 209
Pim    ... want=None on 204 of 209 samples
```

Ten ingots reached the drop in 1800 ticks. Pim crafted once, sold once, earned 35 gold and
then had nothing to do for the rest of the day. **This is the silent-miner shape for the
FOURTH recorded time** (2026-07-30, 2026-08-03, §22.4, here) — and for the second time the
alarm caught it correctly, firing three times and naming `mine_smelt_deliver`.

`NO-TOOL` never fired and `deaths=0` throughout, so it is neither a lost pickaxe nor a
corpse. That leaves vein exhaustion or a relocation that never found live rock — which is
exactly the distinction §9 says the tape still cannot make, now blocking a third
measurement rather than merely being untidy.

### 24.4 Ruled out rather than assumed: not a schema-27 regression

A collapsed run immediately after a contract bump has an obvious suspect, so it was checked
rather than reasoned about. All three buy gates re-run at 27:

| gate | result |
|---|---|
| `live_buy_goal` | PASSED, `iron=0->15 gold=200->125` |
| `live_toolbuy_goal` | PASSED, `tools=0->1 gold=100->87` |
| `live_buy_giveup_gate` | PASSED, `rerolls=4/4 cancels=5 retired=(1,'buy_ingots',21,180,'giveup')` |

The give-up gate's verdict is **byte-identical across schema 26 and 27**, which is the
determinism §21.1 argued makes these gates detectors. The contract is sound; the day was
mining.

### 24.5 Standing state of follow-up 25

**Offline only, still.** Two live attempts at the 49→9 figure: one blocked by the schema
drift, one by a miner that produced ten ingots. The mechanism is mutation-checked at three
levels offline and has never been exercised on a shard.

**And the binding constraint is now named.** Three of the last four forge days were decided
by the miner, not by anything in the tinker's economy. Measuring any further tinker-side
change on a forge day means first making the miner's output reliable — or measuring it
somewhere the miner is not in the loop. Follow-up 27.

1542 tests, ruff clean. Schema 26 → **27**.

## 25. Follow-up 27 — the miner's pool was empty, not thin (2026-08-10)

Three of the last four forge days were decided by the miner. §24.5 named that as the
binding constraint: measuring anything tinker-side on a forge day is pointless while the
supply side decides the outcome.

### 25.1 Diagnosed from the logs before anything was changed

Both 1800-tick days, read side by side:

| | 2026-08-10 (§22) | 2026-08-10 (§24) |
|---|---|---|
| miner's `eps` frozen from | sample 119 / 209 | sample 114 / 209 |
| phase during the stalled tail | `mine` × 90, nothing else | `mine` × 95, nothing else |
| relocation window at the end | `win=23/23` | `win=21/21` |
| pool stands actually stood on | **6 of 6** | **6 of 6** |
| final tile | (2604, 499) | (2604, 499) |

`win=N/N` is every swing in the window returning a no-metal verdict. He relocated, arrived,
and the new rock was dead too — on every spot he had. Both runs ended parked on the same
tile. **The pool was not thin; it was EMPTY.**

`NO-TOOL` never fired and `deaths=0` throughout, so it was neither a lost pickaxe nor a
corpse. That is the vein-versus-relocation distinction §9 says the tape cannot make —
except here the logs settle it by elimination, because "stood on all six" is visible.

### 25.2 The cause was a cap, and the survey had already found the fix

`find_mine_spots` uses `spacing=8` so each stand is one 8×8 `HarvestBank` — 10-34 ore. The
pool was capped at **six**. And the survey at its default radius returns **twelve**: half
the rock the miner had already paid a survey for was being discarded.

```
cap= 6:  6 stands,  6 distinct harvest banks, 58 mineable tiles in reach
cap=12: 11 stands, 11 distinct harvest banks, 94 mineable tiles in reach
```

Twelve doubles the banks (11 + home against 5 + home) and is not an invented number: it is
*everything the existing survey finds*. Raising it needed no wider radius and no new
machinery.

**This is the lumberjack's lesson arriving late.** The constant immediately below
`MINE_POOL_SPOTS` in the same file already records thin Minoc woods as "the size that runs
dry mid-session and leaves `Harvest` relocating instead of working" — diagnosed for one
profession, fixed there by moving to richer woods, and never connected to the miner
standing three lines away.

### 25.3 A correction I made to my own justification

The first version of the comment said the survey is "NEAREST-FIRST", and a test asserted
the pooled stands' distances were monotone. They are not: 0, 8, 16, **13**, 24, 22, …

`find_mine_spots` orders by **BFS depth over walkable non-mine land**, not straight line —
so a stand that is a shorter WALK can be further as the crow flies where the face bends.
The walk is what a relocation hop actually pays, so the walk is the right metric, and the
BFS is also forge12's same-flank filter (the greedy walker wedging into the mountain and
burning the whole pool without arriving). The test now asserts the distances are NOT
monotone, which would catch a switch back to naive ordering.

Asserting the wrong metric would have pinned a property the code does not have and does
not want.

### 25.4 CORRECTED BY §26 — the diagnosis above is wrong

**Read §26 before acting on this section.** The live run that followed put eleven stands in
the pool and the miner stood on SEVEN, leaving four unreached — so "stood on all 6 of 6"
was a consequence of having six, not evidence that six was the limit. The freeze point did
not move. The pool change is kept (it is free and it exposed the real constraint), but the
cause is the cost of PROVING a stand dead, not running out of stands.

### 25.5 What this does NOT fix

- **Untested live.** The pool change has not run on a shard. What it buys is more banks,
  not a guarantee: a 12-bank pool exhausts too, just later.
- **Nothing detects an exhausted POOL.** When all 12 are dead the miner will churn exactly
  as it did on six — relocating between dead stands, `win=N/N`, producing nothing. The
  work-liveness alarm still catches it, which is how this was found, but the agent has no
  "everything I know about is empty, wait for respawn" behaviour. That is the real
  follow-up and it is not this one — **follow-up 28**.
- **Respawn timing is unmeasured.** ServUO restocks ore banks on a timer nobody here has
  measured, so whether 12 banks lasts an 1800-tick day, or whether the first bank has
  refilled by the time the twelfth is dry, is a guess.
- **It does not narrow §9's blind spot.** The tape still cannot distinguish a dead vein
  from a failed relocation; the logs settled it by elimination this time, which will not
  always be available.

1542 → **1545 tests**, ruff clean.

## 26. The pool change, live — and §25's diagnosis was wrong (2026-08-10)

Same command as §22 and §24, `MINE_POOL_SPOTS` 6 → 12. The prediction written beforehand
asked for the miner's freeze point to MOVE LATER. **It did not, and that refutes the
diagnosis rather than the fix.**

| run | miner `eps` | froze at | `!stalled` | pool | stands stood on | banked | net |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| §22 (pool 6) | 69 | 119/209 | 59/209 | 6 | **6 of 6** | 503 | +585 |
| §24 (pool 6) | 72 | 114/209 | 64/209 | 6 | **6 of 6** | 0 | +35 |
| new (pool 12) | 77 | **115/207** | 64/207 | 11 | **7 of 11** | 573 | +655 |

### 26.1 The pool was never the binding constraint

§25.1 read "stood on all 6 of 6" as proof the pool was exhausted. With eleven available he
stood on **seven and stopped** — four stands never reached, and he was still walking
outward when the run ended (his final tile IS pool stand #7). **"Stood on all of them" was
a consequence of having only six, not evidence that six was the limit.** That is a
correlation I read as a cause, and the larger pool is what exposed it.

What the tail actually shows is that he never stopped relocating: five distinct positions
after the freeze, and the relocation window resetting and refilling each time — `20/23`,
then `2/2`, `6/6`, `11/11`, `15/15`, `19/19`. Every sample of every fresh window is a
no-metal verdict. He arrives, proves the spot dead over a full window, walks on, and finds
the next one dead too.

**So the constraint is the COST OF PROVING A SPOT DEAD.** This file already records the
number: the stuck window is `probe_offsets × stuck_window_rotations` = 24 swing replies,
which `test_the_forge_miner_relocates_off_a_dead_vein` pins and which the module comment
calls "roughly two minutes of swinging at a dead vein" at live cadence. Seven dead stands
is most of an hour of the run spent establishing emptiness. More stands does not help when
the miner cannot reach them.

### 26.2 The economics improved and I cannot attribute it

573 banked / +655 net is the best of the three days, against 503/+585 and 0/+35. It is
tempting to call that the pool change working.

**It is not attributable.** The two pool-6 runs differ from each other by more (503 vs 0
banked) than the new run differs from the better of them (503 vs 573). With one sample per
configuration and that much between-run variance, the honest statement is that the change
did not HARM the economy and nothing more.

### 26.3 What is actually true after this run

- **The freeze is not pool exhaustion.** Confirmed by four unreached stands.
- **It is not a lost pickaxe** (`NO-TOOL` never fired) and **not a death** (`deaths=0`),
  which the previous days already established.
- **The ore in reach is consumed faster than the miner can find more**, and each failed
  search costs a full 24-sample window plus a walk.
- **The larger pool is still worth keeping**: it is free (the survey already found the
  stands), it cannot starve a run that would otherwise have reached its sixth stand, and
  it is what made the real constraint visible.

### 26.4 Follow-up 28, restated with what this run taught

The original wording — "nothing detects an exhausted POOL" — is now the wrong shape. The
miner does not exhaust its pool; it exhausts its DAY proving stands dead one 24-sample
window at a time. Two directions, neither guessed at here:

- **Prove a stand dead faster.** 24 samples is the window that avoids relocating off a
  vein that is merely unlucky (`test_a_mostly_dead_vein_still_relocates_despite_trickle_
  successes` is why it is a rate over a window and not a streak). A *100%*-stuck window
  could give up sooner without touching the trickle case — but the number would have to be
  derived and measured, not picked.
- **Notice that the whole AREA is dead and wait.** ServUO respawns ore banks on a timer
  this project has never measured. A miner that waits where it stands, when every recent
  stand has been empty, may out-produce one that walks a mile to prove the same thing.

Measuring either needs the respawn interval, which is the first thing to find out and is
not currently known.

1545 tests, ruff clean.

## 27. The ore respawn interval, measured — and it settles follow-up 28's design (2026-08-10)

§26.4 said measuring either candidate fix "needs the respawn interval, which is the first
thing to find out and is not currently known". It is knowable without spending a minute of
shard time: the shard's own source is in `../servuo`.

### 27.1 The numbers, from `Scripts/Services/Harvest/Mining.cs`

```
oreAndStone.BankWidth  = 8      oreAndStone.BankHeight = 8
oreAndStone.MinTotal   = 10     oreAndStone.MaxTotal   = 34
oreAndStone.MinRespawn = 10 min oreAndStone.MaxRespawn = 20 min
```

The 8×8 bank and the 10-34 ore both confirm what `find_mine_spots`' docstring already
assumed, which is worth noting: that part was right.

**And the timer's start point is the finding.** From `HarvestBank.Consume`, the respawn
clock starts on the FIRST consumption from a full bank — not when the bank empties:

```csharp
if (this.m_Current == this.m_Maximum) {          // first bite out of a full bank
    ...
    this.m_NextRespawn = DateTime.UtcNow + TimeSpan.FromMinutes(minutes);
}
```

So a bank the miner opens at tick T refills at T + respawn regardless of how fast he
drains it. Mining faster does not delay the refill; it just means longer spent empty.

### 27.2 In this project's units

The forge pair's own wall clock, measured off the run: 1800 ticks over 206 status samples
at 4.0 s each = **13.7 minutes, 2.18 ticks/second**.

| | minutes | ticks at 2.18/s |
|---|---|---|
| ore respawn | 10-20 | **1311-2621** |
| the whole forge day | 13.7 | 1800 |

**A bank's respawn is comparable to, or longer than, the entire run.** Only the very first
bank opened has any chance of coming back before the day ends, and only at the fast end of
the range. Every later bank is gone for good.

### 27.3 This settles follow-up 28, and it kills the option I would have reached for

§26.4 named two directions. The measurement decides between them:

- **"Notice the area is dead and wait for respawn" is WRONG** — decisively. The wait is
  1311-2621 ticks against an 1800-tick day. A miner that waits produces nothing for most
  of a run and then maybe gets one bank back. It is the intuitive fix and it is the worse
  one, which is exactly why the number had to be measured before any code.
- **"Reach more banks per day" is the only lever.** Total ore available is
  `banks reached × 10-34`, and nothing refills inside a day, so output is bounded by
  banks reached and by nothing else.

**Which reframes what the 24-sample window costs.** Measured off the same run: production
stopped at t=1026, the run ended at t=1800, and in those **774 dead ticks the miner reached
4 more stands** — about **193 ticks per stand proven dead**, walk included. At that price a
1800-tick day can prove roughly 9 stands empty; the run reached 7. Halving the price would
roughly double the banks reached, and banks reached is the whole output.

### 27.4 What is still NOT known

- **The window's floor.** 24 samples at a 0.75 rate threshold exists so a merely-unlucky
  vein is not abandoned (`test_a_mostly_dead_vein_still_relocates_despite_trickle_
  successes`). What a *100%*-stuck window could safely give up at is unmeasured, and
  picking it without measurement is the mistake §25 already made once.
- **How much of the 193 ticks is walking versus swinging.** The split decides whether the
  lever is the window or the route, and the status line does not separate them.
- **Whether the first bank ever does come back.** At the 10-minute end it would refill
  around tick 1311, inside the run — never observed, because by then the miner has walked
  away and nothing sends him back.
- **`RandomizeVeins = Core.ML`** re-rolls the vein type on respawn, so a refilled bank is
  not necessarily the same ore. Irrelevant to iron-only mining today; relevant the moment
  anything cares which ore it gets.

1545 tests, ruff clean. No shard time spent.

## 28. Walk versus swing, measured — the window is the lever (2026-08-10)

§27.4 left two things unknown and said the walk/swing split "decides whether the lever is
the window or the route". It is measured now, and it is not close.

### 28.1 The instrument, and the derivation that was wrong

`steps=` on the worker line reads **ZERO** across every dead tail, which looks like "no
walking at all" and is an artefact: it counts `Walk` actions, and a relocation issues one
fire-and-forget `WalkTo` and then IDLES while the server advances the route. Position
changes; `steps` does not move. A first attempt to derive the split from it was discarded
for exactly that reason.

So two counters in `Harvest.step`, at the one branch that separates the states, surfaced as
`walk=N swing=M` on the forge pair's `grimm[…]` group. The walk counter increments only
while `_relocate_step` is still returning an action — incrementing before the call put the
ARRIVAL tick, where it returns `None` and execution falls through, into BOTH buckets, and
the two stopped summing to the ticks elapsed. A split that does not partition is not a
split; the test that pins the partition is what caught it.

### 28.2 The answer

```
WHOLE RUN : walk=99  swing=1387   ->  6.7% walking
DEAD TAIL : walk=44  swing=711    ->  5.8% walking
```

In the 755 dead ticks the miner proved **8 stands** empty — about **94 ticks per stand**,
of which roughly **88 are swinging and 5 are walking**.

**The route is not the bottleneck and cannot become one.** Making relocation instant would
recover under 6% of the dead time. The 24-reply stuck window governs the other 94%.

That also revises §27.3's estimate downward: 94 ticks per stand here against the ~193
measured on the previous run, because that figure divided total dead ticks by *position
changes*, and this one counts distinct stands over a longer tail. Same conclusion, better
number.

### 28.3 What is now the actual open question

The window is `probe_offsets × stuck_window_rotations` = 24 replies at a 0.75 rate
threshold, and it is 24 for a reason: `test_a_mostly_dead_vein_still_relocates_despite_
trickle_successes` exists because a strict streak was defeated by rare trickle-through
successes. A *100%*-stuck window is a different case from a 75%-stuck one and could give
up sooner without touching the trickle vein — but **what "sooner" may safely be is
unmeasured**, and picking it by eye is precisely the mistake §25 made with the pool and
§26 had to retract.

The cheap next measurement is the distribution of stuck-rate over the window on a live
run — how often a window that reaches 100% at sample k would have recovered by sample 24.
That is a logging change, not a behaviour change.

### 28.4 The economics, and why I am not claiming them

| run | banked | net |
|---|---|---|
| §22 (pool 6) | 503 | +585 |
| §24 (pool 6) | 0 | +35 |
| §26 (pool 12) | 573 | +655 |
| this run (pool 12) | **818** | **+900** |

The best day this project has recorded, and the second-best also on the larger pool. It is
still not attributable: the two pool-6 runs differ from each other by 503 banked, which is
larger than the gap between the two pool-12 runs. Four runs, two configurations, and a
between-run spread that swamps the difference. The honest reading remains §26.2's — the
pool change did no harm, and may have helped.

The miner froze at sample 116 of 205 again, which is the fourth run in a row at essentially
the same point and the thing none of this has moved.

1547 tests, ruff clean.

## 29. The stuck-streak distribution, measured — and the trickle it guards against never happened (2026-08-10)

§28.3 named the measurement: "how often a window that reaches 100% at sample k would have
recovered by sample 24". Instrumented as a `{streak length: recoveries}` histogram and run
for a full 1800-tick day.

### 29.1 The result

```
recoveries recorded across the whole day : 0
full windows (>=20 samples)              : 21
  ...that were 100% stuck                : 4
  ...containing BOTH kinds               : 7
walk=98 swing=1414   (6.5% walking, consistent with §28)
```

**Not one vein ever produced again after a no-metal reply at the same stand.** The seven
mixed windows are not counter-examples: a window is the last 24 samples, so a stand whose
history runs productive-then-stuck shows both kinds while the window straddles the
transition. Zero recoveries says the ordering is monotone — ore, then nothing, never back.

### 29.2 Why that is stronger than it first looks

A `0` sample is not "the tick went fine". In outcome-only mode — which `Mine` uses — the
sampler's own docstring defines it as *"a productive reply (success, or skill-fail-with-ore
— proof the bank still yields)"*, and everything else, including invalid-target replies,
appends a `1`. So a `0` is direct evidence the bank still has ore, and zero of them ever
followed a `1` at the same stand.

**Which means the trickle case the 24-window exists for did not occur.** And the docstring
explains why it once did: the LEGACY sampler appended a `0` on "every reply-less or
unrecognized tick", so invalid tiles and server-lag silence both read as "not stuck" —
a fully dead bank "crawled at rate ~0.5 and needed 140+ ticks to cross the threshold". The
strict streak that was defeated was a streak over NOISY samples. Outcome-only sampling
removed the noise in 2026-07-30 and nothing re-examined the window it was chosen for.

### 29.3 What this supports, and what it does not

Supported: **a much shorter give-up looks safe for mining** — plausibly even a strict
streak, since under outcome-only sampling an invalid tile extends a streak rather than
breaking it, and a break requires actual ore.

Not supported, and the reason no threshold is changed here:

- **One day, one mine.** 21 full windows is a small sample of vein behaviour, and the mine
  is a single ServUO ore bank layout.
- **The histogram cannot see a recovery ACROSS stands**, by design (§ the reset), so it says
  nothing about whether a stand revisited later would yield — which is the respawn question
  §27 answered separately and negatively for a single day.
- **No max-streak high-water mark was recorded.** "Zero recoveries" and "the longest streak
  reached N" together would be an airtight statement; only the first was instrumented, so
  the strongest available claim is the absence of counter-examples rather than a measured
  bound.

Changing the window on this evidence would repeat §25 exactly: a plausible mechanism, one
run, and a number chosen rather than derived. The next step is a second day plus the
high-water mark — cheap, and it turns "no counter-examples" into a bound.

### 29.4 An instrument bug the first live sample caught

The histogram's first live reading was `recov=24(1)` — a recovery from a streak of exactly
the window length. A number landing precisely on the window is a number to distrust, and it
was an artefact: `_relocate_step` resets `harvest_recent_stuck` and did not reset the new
streak, so a run of stuck replies spanned STANDS — accumulated at a dead vein, carried
across the walk, broken by the first swing at the next one. That measures "how many stuck
replies before some vein anywhere paid" when the question is "how dead did THIS vein look
before IT came back".

Caught by the value, not by a test; there is a test now, and the run carrying the bad
instrument was killed rather than left to produce unusable numbers.

### 29.5 The day itself

`banked=473 net=+550`, miner froze at sample 95 of 208 with `eps=54`. Worse than the
previous day's 818/+900 on identical settings, which is the fifth data point on how wide
the between-run spread is (0, 473, 503, 573, 818 banked across five 1800-tick days) and a
standing reason not to attribute any of it.

1551 tests, ruff clean.

## 30. The streak bound, measured — and a new sell failure the same run exposed (2026-08-11)

### 30.1 The bound §29.3 said was missing

`peak=` records the longest stuck streak reached anywhere in a run, deliberately NOT reset
by a relocation — the opposite rule to the streak itself, which must reset or it measures
two veins as one. Full 1800-tick day:

```
recov=none peak=24        peak values as the day progressed: 5, 10, 14, 19, 23, 24
recoveries anywhere       : 0
walk=109 swing=1351         (7.5% walking — consistent with §28's 6.7%)
```

**Streaks reached 24 — the full window — and not one of any length ever broke.** Two
consecutive days now with zero recoveries, and this one bounds the claim: every streak
length from 1 to 24 was reached, so the absence is not for want of opportunity.

Combined with §29.2's semantics — a `0` sample means *"a productive reply … proof the bank
still yields"*, and invalid tiles append `1` — the statement is: **at this mine, once a
stand starts replying "no metal", real ore never appears again at that stand.** A give-up
at any threshold ≤ 24 would have abandoned nothing that would have paid, across two days.

Still not changed, and the reason is unchanged: two days at one mine, one ore type, one
`probe_offsets` ring. The evidence now supports the change; what it does not yet do is
bound the risk at a *different* mine or for `Chop`/`Fish`, which share `Harvest` and whose
windows the same constant governs. A third profession's day would settle that, and it is
cheap.

### 30.2 The same run lost its whole economy to something else

```
retired>=128:128g   (203 sell_tongs give-ups, EVERY ONE at age 8)
banked=0  net=+0g   tongs=5 in the pack at the end
sell_stage           : never printed once
```

Pim crafted, then tried to sell **203 times** and never got past the first stage. He
ended the day holding 5 tongs and no gold.

**Corrected by §31:** this section first said "`mkt_phase` never became `sell`". It did —
134 samples show `mkt_phase=sell`. The claim was inferred from `sell_stage` never printing,
which is a weaker fact than it looks: `sell_stage` is only WRITTEN once the trip's walk
completes, so its absence means the trip died before its first stage rather than never
starting.

What is different about this run's staging, and it is the only difference visible:

```
this run    : Tinker at (2611,473), 2 from the stand   (leash 2)
previous run: Tinker at (2610,474), 1 from the stand   (leash 1)
```

The Tinker landed on a different tile — and (2611,473) is **adjacent to Grimm's own stand
at (2611,474)**. That is the shape of a failure this project already paid for (a
nearest-mobile resolver picking the wrong body), which is exactly why `shop_serials` exists.

**But the identity pin IS wired here** — `run_forge_pair` sets `m["shop_serials"]` on both
of Pim's memories — so the obvious explanation is ruled out and the cause is unknown. It is
recorded as a finding, not diagnosed, because guessing at it is what §25 did.

**Follow-up 29: `sell_tongs` gives up at age 8 without entering its FSM.** What is known:
203 occurrences in one day, always age 8, `sell_stage` absent throughout, `ui=gump` on
exactly one sample, the vendor staged 2 tiles from the stand rather than 1, and the shop
identity pin present. What to check first is whether `_begin_goal` is refusing — that is
the one path that no-ops without ever setting `mkt_phase`, and it refuses when the route
cannot be built from `vendor_spot`.

### 30.3 Two things this run says about earlier work

- **Follow-up 19 earned its keep, visibly.** 203 sell frames retired through bound 1 at age
  8. Before that marker they would each have burned a 180-tick deadline, and 203 × 180 is
  many times the run — the day would have shown a handful of enormous stalls instead of a
  clear signal repeated 203 times. The give-up marker is what made this diagnosable at all.
- **The economics remain unattributable, now with six samples**: 0, 473, 503, 573, 818 and
  now 0 banked across six 1800-tick days on the same command. Any claim about a change's
  effect on gold needs a design that survives that spread, and none of the runs so far is
  one.

1553 tests, ruff clean.

## 31. Follow-up 29 diagnosed as far as the evidence allows (2026-08-11)

### 31.1 A correction to §30.2 first

That section said the sell trip "never got past the first stage — `mkt_phase` never became
`sell`". The second half is wrong: **134 samples show `mkt_phase=sell`.** The claim was
inferred from `sell_stage` never printing, and that is a weaker fact than it looks —
`sell_stage` is only WRITTEN once `_walk_route` returns `_ARRIVED`, so its absence means
the trip **started and died inside its walk**, not that it never started.

Which relocates the whole search: the failure is in the walk to the vendor, not in the sell
FSM.

### 31.2 Three hypotheses eliminated by offline reproduction

The MockBody vendor built for follow-up 22 pays for itself here. Driving a real
`TinkerLife` at the failing run's exact geometry — Pim at (2609,474), Tinker at (2611,473),
`SELL_REACH=2` so he is already in reach:

| world | result |
|---|---|
| bare (no miner, identity pin set) | reaches `stage=popup`, `vendor=0xbeef` |
| **Grimm adjacent to the vendor tile**, pin set | reaches `stage=popup`, `vendor=0xbeef` |
| Grimm adjacent, **no identity pin at all** | reaches `stage=popup`, `vendor=0xbeef` |

**NARROWED BY §32 — this table proves less than it was written to prove.** `MockBody`'s
default `bounds` were `(0, 0, 1000, 1000)` and every coordinate here is real UO ground near
(2611,474), so every `Walk` in that fixture was refused as an edge bump and **the agent
could not walk at all**. All three variants "succeeded" only because Pim starts inside
`SELL_REACH` and needed no walk.

What the table therefore does establish: **vendor RESOLUTION is not the cause** — the
resolver returns the Tinker's serial in all three worlds, pinned or not, so §30.2's
"nearest-mobile resolver picked the wrong body" is genuinely refuted.

What it does NOT establish, contrary to the original wording: anything about the WALK,
which is exactly where §31.1 places the failure. A blocked approach was untestable in that
fixture by construction.

### 31.3 Why a full day's log could not narrow it further, and the one-line fix

The pair line's capability-stage group prints `sell_stage`, `sell_vendor`, `sell_find_wait`
— every key the sell FSM writes AFTER its walk — and nothing the walk itself writes. So a
trip that dies in the walk produces a line that is blank in exactly the region where the
answer is.

`sell_leg` and `buy_leg` are in that list now. It is the same lesson the block already
carries in its own comment — *"an ADMITTED goal that does not progress is invisible … so
the FSM has to say where it is stuck itself"* — applied one stage earlier than it was
written for, because the earlier stage had never been the one that failed.

### 31.4 What is now known, and what the next run should answer

Established: the trip starts, dies inside `_walk_route` at age 8, does so 203 times in a
day, and is not caused by geometry, by the adjacent miner, or by the identity pin.

Unresolved, and the next run's job: whether `sell_leg` advances at all. If it never
appears, `_walk_route` is wedging on its first leg — `_market_walk_toward`'s stall-giveup,
which would point at a blocked approach the mock cannot model (a real NPC occupying a tile,
or terrain). If it advances and then stops, the give-up is elsewhere in the walk.

**Not fixed, and deliberately.** Every candidate remaining is one the mock cannot reproduce,
so any change would be guessed rather than measured — which is what §25 did with the mine
pool and §26 had to retract. One more day with `sell_leg` on the line costs nothing extra
and turns the remaining suspects into one.

1553 tests, ruff clean.

## 32. The reproduction that could not walk (2026-08-11)

§31.2 reported three hypotheses "eliminated" by an offline reproduction. The reproduction
could not move.

`MockBody.bounds` defaulted to `(0, 0, 1000, 1000)`, and every production coordinate in
this project is real UO ground — the trade mine is ~(2611,474), the Yew woods ~(2520,450).
`_walk` refuses any step outside the box as an edge bump, silently, exactly as it refuses a
wall. So a fixture built at a real spot never moved, and the three variants all reached
`stage=popup` only because Pim starts inside `SELL_REACH` and needed no walk at all.

**A double whose failure mode is silent immobility teaches the wrong thing twice: once when
it passes, and once when the pass is believed.** §31.2 is corrected in place rather than
deleted — it still refutes the vendor-resolution hypothesis, because the resolver returned
the right serial in every variant, and that part never depended on walking.

### 32.1 With the bounds fixed, the telemetry choice checks out

Re-run at the same geometry with `bounds` spanning the real map:

| world | `sell_stage` | `sell_stall` peak | ended at |
|---|---|---|---|
| already in reach | `popup` | 2 | moved off |
| far, CLEAR path | — | **0** | advanced to (2606,474) |
| far, WEDGED behind a wall | — | **5** | stuck at (2604,474) |

`stall_limit` is 6, so a wedge sits just under it while a clear walk never accumulates.
**Walking and wedged are distinguishable, and `{tag}_leg` cannot distinguish them** —
`_walk_route` returns `_ARRIVED` before touching the leg cursor, and on the single-waypoint
routes `stage_shops` produces, leg 0 is also the last leg, so the key is never written on
any path. That was caught by noticing `sell_leg` absent on a run where the sell was
WORKING, before a second live day was spent on it.

The status line carries `sell_stall`/`buy_stall` now, not the leg.

### 32.2 The default is fixed, not worked around

`bounds` now spans UO's map. Two tests pin it: one walks at (2609,474) and asserts the
position changes, one asserts a wedged market walk is distinguishable from a walking one.
Both fail under the old 1000×1000 default.

Fixing the default rather than passing `bounds=` per fixture is deliberate — the trap is
that it is silent, and every future fixture at real coordinates would have hit it.

### 32.3 Follow-up 29 is still open, and the run that was supposed to close it did not

The Tinker staged at (2610,473), one tile from the stand — not the (2611,473) the failure
needed. The sell worked (`sell_stage=list`), so the day says nothing about follow-up 29,
which the written-beforehand prediction named as the likeliest outcome. Placement is the
server's, not ours.

What is now in place for when it recurs: `sell_stall` on the line, and a fixture that can
actually walk. What is still unknown is unchanged from §31.4 — whether the walk wedges, and
if so on what.

1553 → **1555 tests**, ruff clean.

## 33. Follow-up 25, live at last — 49 hopeless trips become 5 (2026-08-11)

Two earlier attempts failed to measure this: one lost to schema drift, one to a miner that
never earned enough to want a buy at all (§24.2). This day got it, incidentally, while
looking for something else.

### 33.1 The comparison, and why these two runs ARE comparable

| | §22 (no backoff) | this run (backoff) |
|---|---|---|
| `buy_iron` retirements, in order | achieved, achieved, then **49 give-ups** | achieved, achieved, then **5 give-ups** |
| samples with `buy_iron` admitted | 60 | **11** |

**The shape is identical**: the vendor sold its stock to exactly two purchases and then had
none, in both runs, with every subsequent trip a correct discovery that there was nothing
to buy. That is what makes the pair comparable in the way that matters — unlike the gold
figures, which §30.3 records as unattributable across six days. The question here is not
"did this day earn more" but "how many times did the tinker walk to a shop it had just
proved empty", and both runs answer it under the same world condition.

**49 → 5.** The prediction from the offline Life was 9; the live number is lower, plausibly
because the tinker spent part of the day on delivered iron and so was not continuously
wanting to buy. Predicted direction and rough magnitude, better outcome.

### 33.2 The rest of the day

- **Third consecutive day of `recov=none peak=24`.** Streaks reach the full window, no vein
  of any length ever recovers. Three days now.
- **walk/swing 99/1311 = 7.0% walking**, against 6.7% and 7.5% on the previous two. The
  ~93% swinging figure is stable across three days.
- **The miner froze at sample 121/207, 58% through** — the fifth consecutive run at
  essentially the same point, and still the thing nothing has moved.
- Economy healthy and unremarkable: 19 crafts, 19 sells, 15 banks, `banked=503 net=+585g`,
  zero deaths, zero `!frozen`, zero `FRAME OVERDUE`, zero rule-vs-gate disagreements.
- **Follow-up 29 did not recur** — the Tinker staged one tile from the stand rather than
  two, the sell worked, and the day says nothing about it. Named in the prediction as the
  likeliest outcome.

### 33.3 What this does and does not settle

Settled: the vendor-dry backoff works on a shard, on the flagship chain, under the same
world condition that produced the 49.

Not settled:

- **One live pair of runs.** Two days, one configuration each, and the runs differ in
  everything except the dry-vendor shape.
- **`VENDOR_DRY_BACKOFF = 180` is still only derived**, not tuned. §27 measured the ore
  respawn at 10-20 minutes but ServUO's *vendor* restock interval is a different number and
  remains unmeasured, so whether 180 ticks is short enough to catch a restock is unknown.
- **The tool-buy family still marks a dry vendor with no rule reading it** (follow-up 26).

1555 tests, ruff clean.

## 34. The miner's five-run freeze, diagnosed — and the hypothesis I went in with, refuted (2026-08-12)

Method note, because it is part of the finding. Four open items were investigated in
parallel, each by an independent reader, and each finding was then attacked by two
skeptics with different briefs: one re-ran the cited evidence, one granted the evidence
and attacked the causal story. **Every one of the four headline claims was damaged**, and
three of the four had their proposed FIX refuted while their diagnosis survived. That
asymmetry — the mechanism is right, the repair is wrong — is the single most useful thing
the exercise produced, and it is why this section ships three verified defects and none of
the four fixes originally proposed for them.

### 34.1 My own hypothesis, refuted by its own arithmetic
